be a Jordan curve in
(\mathbb{P},V)
parametrized as a simple loop
\beta\colon\mathbb{I}\rightarrow\mathbb{P}
, and let
\alpha\colon\mathbb{I}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
be a lift of
\beta
under
\wp
.

(i)

If
\alpha(0)=\alpha(1)
and
\alpha
is a path in a convex subset of
\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, then
\gamma
is null-homotopic in
(\mathbb{P},V)
.

(ii)

If
\gamma
is essential, then
\alpha(1)-\alpha(0)
is a primitive element of
2\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. It is uniquely determined up to sign by the isotopy class

[\gamma]
of
\gamma
rel.
V
.

Here we call
\alpha
a
lift
of
\beta
under
\wp
if
\beta=\wp\circ\alpha
.
In this lemma and its proof, we will carefully distinguish between a
path and its image set (unlike elsewhere in the paper).

Proof.

Note that since
\beta(\mathbb{I})=\gamma\subset\mathbb{P}\setminus V
and
\wp
is a covering map over

\mathbb{P}\setminus V
, a lift
\alpha
of
\beta
under
\wp

exists. Moreover, for each choice of
z_{0}\in\wp^{-1}(\beta(0))
, there exists a unique lift
\alpha
of
\beta
such that
\alpha(0)=z_{0}

(for these standard facts see
[
Hat02
, Section 1.3, Proposition 1.30]
).
We will use this uniqueness property of lifts repeatedly in the following.

(i)
The idea for the first part is very simple. We use a “straight-line homotopy” between
\alpha
and the constant path
u\in\mathbb{I}\mapsto\alpha(0)
and push it to
\mathbb{P}\setminus V
by applying
\wp
.

More precisely, we define

H(u,t)\coloneq\wp((1-t)\alpha(u)+t\alpha(0))

for
u,t\in\mathbb{I}
. Since the path
\alpha
lies in a convex set
K\subset\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, we have

\alpha_{t}(u)\coloneq(1-t)\alpha(u)+t\alpha(0)\in K

for all
u,t\in\mathbb{I}
, and so
H(\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I})\subset\wp(\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{Z}^{2})=\mathbb{P}\setminus V
. Hence
H
is a homotopy in
\mathbb{P}\setminus V
. Moreover,
H_{t}(u)=(\wp\circ\alpha_{t})(u)
for all
u\in\mathbb{I}
, and so
H_{t}=\wp\circ\alpha_{t}
for all
t\in\mathbb{I}
.
In particular,
H_{0}=\wp\circ\alpha_{0}=\wp\circ\alpha=\beta
. Moreover,
H_{1}(u)=\alpha(0)
for
u\in\mathbb{I}
, and so
H_{1}
is a constant path.

For all
t\in\mathbb{I}
we have

\alpha_{t}(1)-\alpha_{t}(0)=(1-t)(\alpha(1)-\alpha(0))=0.

Hence
\wp(\alpha_{t}(1))=\wp(\alpha_{t}(0))
for
t\in\mathbb{I}
, and so

H_{t}=\wp\circ\alpha_{t}
is a loop in
\mathbb{P}\setminus V
for all
t\in\mathbb{I}
.
By identifying
the points
(0,t)
and
(1,t)
for each
t\in\mathbb{I}
, we obtain a homotopy

\overline{H}\colon\partial\mathbb{D}\times\mathbb{I}\rightarrow\mathbb{P}\setminus V

such that

\overline{H}(e^{2\pi iu},t)=H(u,t)

for all
u,t\in\mathbb{I}
. Since
\overline{H}_{0}(e^{2\pi iu})=H_{0}(u)=\beta(u)

for
u\in\mathbb{I}
, we see that
\overline{H}_{0}
is a homeomorphism of
\partial\mathbb{D}
onto
\beta(\mathbb{I})=\gamma
. On the other hand,
\overline{H}_{1}
is a constant map.
Hence
\gamma
is null-homotopic in
(\mathbb{P},V)
.

(ii)

The proof is somewhat tedious as we have to worry about different choices of the curve in
[\gamma]
, its different parametrizations as a simple loop, and the different lifts of these parametrizations under
\wp
.

To prove the statement, we first consider a special case, namely we choose a Jordan curve

\gamma_{0}
in
(\mathbb{P},V)
that lies in the same isotopy class
rel.
V
as
\gamma
with the additional property that
\gamma_{0}
is in minimal position with the set
a\cup c
. Since
\gamma_{0}
cannot be a subset of
a\cup c
, we can parametrize
\gamma_{0}
as a simple loop
\beta_{0}\colon\mathbb{I}\rightarrow\mathbb{P}
such that
\beta_{0}(0)=\beta_{0}(1)\not\in a\cup c
. We now consider a lift
\alpha_{0}\colon\mathbb{I}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
of
\beta_{0}
under
\wp
. Note that
\alpha_{0}
is a simple loop or a homeomorphic parametrization on an arc in
\mathbb{C}
.

Since
\gamma
is essential, the Jordan curve
\gamma_{0}
is also essential. Indeed, under an isotopy rel.
V
that deforms
\gamma
into
\gamma_{0}
,
the complementary components of
\gamma
in
\mathbb{P}
are deformed into the complementary components of
\gamma_{0}
while the points in
V
stay fixed. Therefore, each of the two components of
\mathbb{P}\setminus\gamma_{0}
contains precisely two points of
V
.

It follows from Lemma
10.1
that
\gamma_{0}
meets
a
and
c
transversely. Moreover, by Lemma
10.3
either
\gamma_{0}\cap(a\cup c)=\emptyset
, or the sets
a\cap\gamma_{0}
and
c\cap\gamma_{0}
are non-empty and finite, and the points in these sets alternate on
\gamma_{0}
. We now define
\overline{\alpha}_{0}\coloneq\alpha_{0}(\mathbb{I})
. Then
\overline{\alpha}_{0}
is a Jordan curve or an arc in
\mathbb{C}
. Moreover, we either have

\overline{\alpha}_{0}\cap\wp^{-1}(a\cup c)=\emptyset
, or
\overline{\alpha}_{0}
meets each of the lines
in
\wp^{-1}(a\cup c)
transversely,

0<\#(\overline{\alpha}_{0}\cap\wp^{-1}(a))=\#(\overline{\alpha}_{0}\cap\wp^{-1}(c))<\infty,

and the points in
\overline{\alpha}_{0}\cap\wp^{-1}(a)
and

\overline{\alpha}_{0}\cap\wp^{-1}(c)
alternate on

\overline{\alpha}_{0}
. Let
z_{0}\coloneq\alpha_{0}(0)
and

w_{0}\coloneq\alpha_{0}(1)
. Then

\wp(z_{0})=\wp(\alpha_{0}(0))=\beta_{0}(0)=\beta_{0}(1)=\wp(\alpha_{0}(1))=\wp(w_{0}),

and by the choice of
\beta_{0}
we have
z_{0},w_{0}\not\in\wp^{-1}(a\cup c)
. Therefore, we are exactly in the situation of
Lemma
10.4
.

It follows that
v_{0}\coloneq w_{0}-z_{0}\in 2\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. Here
v_{0}\neq 0
. Indeed, otherwise
z_{0}=w_{0}
. Then the second part of Lemma
10.4
implies that the arc
\overline{\alpha}_{0}
does not meet
\wp^{-1}(a\cup c)
and so it lies in a connected component of

\mathbb{C}\setminus\wp^{-1}(a\cup c)
. This component is an infinite
strip, and hence a convex set, contained in
\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.
Now
(i)
implies that
\gamma_{0}
is null-homotopic in
(\mathbb{P},V)
. By Lemma
10.5
this contradicts the fact that
\gamma_{0}
is essential. We conclude that indeed

v_{0}=w_{0}-z_{0}\neq 0
.

This shows that
\alpha_{0}(1)-\alpha_{0}(0)=w_{0}-z_{0}

is a non-zero element of the lattice
2\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. We claim that

w_{0}-z_{0}
is actually a primitive element of
2\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.
To see this, we argue by contradiction and assume that

w_{0}-z_{0}=ny_{0}
with
y_{0}\in 2\mathbb{Z}^{2}\setminus\{0\}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
n\geq 2
.

We now consider the path
\sigma\colon\mathbb{I}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
given as

\sigma(u)=\exp(\frac{2\pi i}{y_{0}}\alpha_{0}(u))
for
u\in\mathbb{I}
.
This is a loop with winding number

\text{ind}_{\sigma}(0)=\frac{1}{y_{0}}(\alpha_{0}(1)-\alpha_{0}(0))=n

around
0
.
Now a simple loop in
\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
has winding number

0
or
\pm 1
around
0
(see
[
Bur79
, Theorem 4.42]
), and so

\sigma
cannot be simple. This implies that there are numbers

0\leq u<u^{\prime}<1
such that
\sigma(u)=\sigma(u^{\prime})
. This in turn means that

\alpha_{0}(u^{\prime})-\alpha_{0}(u)=ky_{0}
for some
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Since
y_{0}\in 2\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, it follows
that

\beta_{0}(u^{\prime})=\wp(\alpha_{0}(u^{\prime}))=\wp(\alpha_{0}(u))=\beta_{0}(u).

This is impossible, since
\beta_{0}
is injective on
[0,1)
.

We have shown the first part of the statement for a particular Jordan curve
\gamma_{0}
in
[\gamma]
with a special parametrization
\beta_{0}
, and a choice of a lift
\alpha_{0}
of
\beta_{0}
under
\wp
. We now have to show that the number
v_{0}=w_{0}-z_{0}=\alpha_{0}(1)-\alpha_{0}(0)
obtained in this way only depends on
[\gamma]
up to sign. For this we pick an arbitrary Jordan curve in
[\gamma]
which we will simply call
\gamma
.

Since
\gamma_{0}\sim\gamma
rel.
V
, there exists an isotopy

H\colon\mathbb{P}\times\mathbb{I}\rightarrow\mathbb{P}
rel.
V
with
H_{0}=\operatorname{id}_{\mathbb{P}}
and

H_{1}(\gamma_{0})=\gamma
.
We now define a homotopy
\overline{H}\colon\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I}\rightarrow\mathbb{P}\setminus V
by setting

\overline{H}(u,t)\coloneqq H(\beta_{0}(u),t)

for
u,t\in\mathbb{I}
.
Note that
\overline{H}
maps into
\mathbb{P}\setminus V

as follows from the facts that
\gamma_{0}\subset\mathbb{P}\setminus V
and
H
is an isotopy rel.
V
.

The time-
0
map
\overline{H}(\cdot,0)=\beta_{0}
of the homotopy
\overline{H}
is the parametrization of the loop
\gamma_{0}
, while
the time-
1
map
\beta\coloneq\overline{H}(\cdot,1)=H_{1}\circ\beta_{0}
gives some parametrization of
\gamma=H_{1}(\gamma_{0})
as a simple loop.

By the homotopy lifting theorem (see
[
Hat02
, Proposition 1.30]
), there exists
a homotopy
\widetilde{H}\colon\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{Z}^{2}
such that

\overline{H}=\wp\circ\widetilde{H}
and
\widetilde{H}_{0}=\widetilde{H}(\cdot,0)=\alpha_{0}
. Then
\alpha\coloneqq\widetilde{H}(\cdot,1)
is a lift of
\beta=\overline{H}(\cdot,1)
under
\wp
. We want to show that
\alpha(1)-\alpha(0)=v_{0}=w_{0}-z_{0}
.

To see this, we consider the paths

\sigma,\tau\colon\mathbb{I}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{Z}^{2}
defined as
\sigma(t)=\widetilde{H}(0,t)

and
\tau(t)=\widetilde{H}(1,t)
for
t\in\mathbb{I}
.
Then

(10.1)

\displaystyle\overline{\sigma}(t)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\wp(\sigma(t))=\wp(\widetilde{H}(0,t))=\overline{H}(0,t)

\displaystyle=H_{t}(\beta(0))=H_{t}(\beta(1))=\overline{H}(1,t)=\wp(\widetilde{H}(1,t))=\wp(\tau(t))

for
t\in\mathbb{I}
. Note also that

\tau(0)=\widetilde{H}(1,0)=\alpha_{0}(1)=\alpha_{0}(0)+v_{0}=\widetilde{H}(0,0)+v_{0}=\sigma(0)+v_{0}.

This implies that the paths
\tau
and
t\in\mathbb{I}\mapsto\sigma(t)+v_{0}
have the same initial points. Since
v_{0}\in 2\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, it follows from (
2.3
) and (
10.1
) that the map
\wp
sends them both to
\overline{\sigma}=\wp\circ\sigma=\wp\circ\tau
, which is a path in
\mathbb{P}\setminus V
. It follows from the uniqueness of lifts under
\wp
that

\tau(t)=\sigma(t)+v_{0}
for all
t\in\mathbb{I}
.

This implies that

\alpha(1)-\alpha(0)=\widetilde{H}(1,1)-\widetilde{H}(0,1)=\tau(1)-\sigma(1)=v_{0},

as desired.

Note that for a given parametrization
\beta
of
\gamma
, the difference
\alpha(1)-\alpha(0)
is independent up to sign of the choice
of the lift
\alpha
of
\beta
. Indeed, suppose
\alpha^{\prime}
is another lift of

\beta
under
\wp
.
Then

\wp(\alpha(0))=\beta(0)=\wp(\alpha^{\prime}(0)),

and so by (
2.3
) we have

\alpha^{\prime}(0)=\pm\alpha(0)+m_{0}

for some (fixed) choice of the sign
\pm
and
m_{0}\in 2\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. Then

t\in\mathbb{I}\mapsto\pm\alpha(t)+m_{0}
is a lift of
\beta
with the same initial point as
\alpha^{\prime}
and so we see that

\alpha^{\prime}(t)=\pm\alpha(t)+m_{0}

for all
t\in\mathbb{I}
. This implies that

(10.2)

\alpha^{\prime}(1)-\alpha^{\prime}(0)=\pm(\alpha(1)-\alpha(0)),

as desired.

It remains to show that up to sign
\alpha(1)-\alpha(0)
is independent of the choice
of the parametrization
\beta
of
\gamma
. For this we consider
another parametrization
\beta^{\prime}
of
\gamma
as a simple loop.
We first assume that
\beta^{\prime}(0)=\beta(0)
.
Then there exists a homeomorphism
h\colon\mathbb{I}\rightarrow\mathbb{I}
such that

\beta^{\prime}=\beta\circ h
. Here
h
fixes the endpoints
0
and
1
of
\mathbb{I}
or interchanges them depending on whether
\beta^{\prime}
parametrizes
\gamma
with the same or opposite orientation as
\beta
, respectively.
In any case,
\alpha^{\prime}=\alpha\circ h
is a lift of
\beta^{\prime}
under
\wp
.
It follows that

\alpha^{\prime}(1)-\alpha^{\prime}(0)=\alpha(h(1))-\alpha(h(0))=\pm(\alpha(1)-\alpha(0)),

as desired. As we now know, this relation is independent of the specific choice of the lift
\alpha^{\prime}
of
\beta^{\prime}
.

Finally, we have to consider the case where
\beta^{\prime}
has a possibly different
initial point than
\beta
, say
p_{0}\coloneq\beta^{\prime}(0)\in\gamma
. By what we have seen, in order to establish (
10.2
), we can choose any
parametrization
\beta^{\prime}
of
\gamma
as a simple loop with
\beta^{\prime}(0)=p_{0}
and any lift
\alpha^{\prime}
of
\beta^{\prime}
.

We can extend our parametrization
\beta
on
\mathbb{I}
periodically to a continuous map
\beta\colon\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\gamma
such that

\beta(u+1)=\beta(u)
for all
u\in\mathbb{R}
. Then
\beta
lifts under

\wp
to a continuous map
\alpha\colon\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
which agrees
with the original lift
\alpha
on
\mathbb{I}
.
Then
u\in\mathbb{R}\mapsto\alpha(u+1)-v_{0}
is also a lift of
\beta
under

\wp
. The initial point of this lift corresponding to
u=0
is equal to
\alpha(0)
. The uniqueness of lifts implies that this lift and the original lift
\alpha
are the same paths and so

(10.3)

\alpha(u+1)=\alpha(u)+v_{0}

for all
u\in\mathbb{R}
.

We can find
u_{0}\in[0,1)
such that
\beta(u_{0})=p_{0}
. Then

\beta^{\prime}\colon\mathbb{I}\rightarrow\gamma
defined as
\beta^{\prime}(u)=\beta(u_{0}+u)
for
u\in\mathbb{I}
is a parametrization of
\gamma
as a simple loop with the initial point
p_{0}
. Under
\wp
this path
\beta^{\prime}
has the lift
\alpha^{\prime}\colon\mathbb{I}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
given by
\alpha^{\prime}(u)=\alpha(u+u_{0})
for
u\in\mathbb{I}
.
Then (
10.3
) implies that

\alpha^{\prime}(1)-\alpha^{\prime}(0)=\alpha(u_{0}+1)-\alpha(u_{0})=v_{0},

as desired. The proof is complete.
∎

We are now almost ready to prove Lemma
2.3
.
Before we get to this, it is useful to discuss an alternative way to view our pillow
\mathbb{P}
.

We consider a slope
r/s\in\widehat{\mathbb{Q}}
. Then we can choose
p,q\in\mathbb{Z}

such
pr+qs=1
and define
\omega\coloneq s+ir
and
\widetilde{\omega}\coloneq-p+iq
. The numbers
\omega
and
\widetilde{\omega}
form a basis of
\mathbb{C}\cong\mathbb{R}^{2}
over
\mathbb{R}
, and so every point
z\in\mathbb{C}
can be uniquely written in the form

z=u\widetilde{\omega}+v\omega
with
u,v\in\mathbb{R}
. Accordingly, the map

z=u\widetilde{\omega}+v\omega\mapsto R(z)\coloneq u\widetilde{\omega}-v\omega\mapsto
for
u,v\in\mathbb{R}
is a well-defined “skew-reflection”
R
on
\mathbb{C}
. Note also that
\mathbb{Z}^{2}=\{n\widetilde{\omega}+k\omega:n,k\in\mathbb{Z}\}
.

We consider the parallelogram
Q\coloneq\{u\widetilde{\omega}+v\omega:u\in[0,1],\,v\in[-1,1]\}\subset\mathbb{C}
.
Then it follows from (
2.3
) that

\wp(Q)=\mathbb{P}
. Moreover, for
z,w\in Q
,
z\neq w
, we have

\wp(z)=\wp(w)
if and only if
z,w\in\partial Q
and
w=R(z)
. Intuitively, this means that the pillow
\mathbb{P}
can be obtained from

Q
by “folding”
Q
in its middle segment
[0,\widetilde{\omega}]\subset Q
and identifying the points on
\partial Q
that correspond to each other under the skew-reflection
R
. The map
\wp
sends
the set
\{0,\widetilde{\omega},\widetilde{\omega}+\omega,\omega\}
bijectively onto the set
\{A,B,C,D\}
of vertices of
\mathbb{P}
(but not necessarily in that order).

From this geometric picture it is clear that for each
t\in(0,1)

the set

\tau_{r/s}\coloneq\wp([t\widetilde{\omega}-\omega,t\widetilde{\omega}+\omega])=\wp(\ell_{r/s}(t\widetilde{\omega}))

is a simple closed geodesic in
(\mathbb{P},V)
. Moreover, the sets

\xi_{r/s}\coloneq\wp([-\omega,+\omega])=\wp(\ell_{r/s}(0))\text{ and }\xi^{\prime}_{r/s}\coloneq\wp([\widetilde{\omega}-\omega,\widetilde{\omega}+\omega])=\wp(\ell_{r/s}(\widetilde{\omega}))

are geodesic core arcs of
\tau_{r/s}
lying in different components of
\mathbb{P}\setminus\tau_{r/s}
. In particular,
\tau_{r/s}
is an essential Jordan curve in
(\mathbb{P},V)
.

It follows from (
2.3
) that

\wp(\ell_{r/s}(t\widetilde{\omega}))\cap\wp(\ell_{r/s}(t^{\prime}\widetilde{\omega}))\neq\emptyset
for
t,t^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
if and only if
t^{\prime}-t\in 2\mathbb{Z}
or
t^{\prime}+t\in 2\mathbb{Z}
. In this case, we have
\wp(\ell_{r/s}(t\widetilde{\omega}))=\wp(\ell_{r/s}(t^{\prime}\widetilde{\omega}))
. Moreover,
\tau=\wp(\ell_{r/s}(t\widetilde{\omega}))
is a simple closed geodesic
\tau_{r/s}
in
(\mathbb{P},V)
if
t\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Z}
, it is
equal to the geodesic arc
\xi_{r/s}
if
t
is an even integer, and is equal to the geodesic arc
\xi^{\prime}_{r/s}

if
t
is an odd integer. Note that
\ell_{r/s}(t\widetilde{\omega})
for
t\in\mathbb{R}
contains a point in
\mathbb{Z}^{2}=\{n\widetilde{\omega}+k\omega:n,k\in\mathbb{Z}\}
if and only if
t\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Lemma 10.7
.

Let
\tau_{r/s}
and
\tau^{\prime}_{r/s}
be two distinct simple closed geodesics in
(\mathbb{P},V)
with slope
r/s\in\widehat{\mathbb{Q}}
. Then

\tau_{r/s}
and
\tau^{\prime}_{r/s}
are isotopic rel.
V
.

Proof.

The previous considerations imply that we may assume that the geodesics are represented in the form

\tau_{r/s}=\wp(\ell_{r/s}(t\widetilde{\omega}))
and

\tau^{\prime}_{r/s}=\wp(\ell_{r/s}(t^{\prime}\widetilde{\omega}))
with

t,t^{\prime}\in(0,1)
,
t\neq t^{\prime}
. We may assume
t<t^{\prime}
. Then

U\coloneq\wp(\{u\widetilde{\omega}+v\omega:u\in(t,t^{\prime}),\,v\in[-1,1]\})

is an annulus contained in
\mathbb{P}\setminus V
with

\partial U=\tau_{r/s}\cup\tau^{\prime}_{r/s}
. It follows from Lemma
2.1
that
\tau_{r/s}
and

\tau^{\prime}_{r/s}
are isotopic rel.
V
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
2.3
.

Let
\gamma
be an essential Jordan curve in
(\mathbb{P},V)
.
If we parametrize
\gamma
as a simple loop
\beta\colon\mathbb{I}\rightarrow\mathbb{P}
and
lift
\beta
to a path
\alpha\colon\mathbb{I}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
under
\wp
, then by Lemma
10.6
we know that
\alpha(1)-\alpha(0)
is a primitive element of
2\mathbb{Z}^{2}
uniquely determined by
[\gamma]
up to sign. Hence we can find relatively prime integers
r,s\in\mathbb{Z}
such that

(10.4)

\alpha(1)-\alpha(0)=2(s+ir).

By switching signs here, which corresponds to parametrizing
\gamma
with opposite orientation,
we may assume that

r\in\mathbb{Z}
,
s\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, and that
r=1
if
s=0
. Note that with these restrictions on
r
and
s
, the primitive element
2(s+ir)
of
2\mathbb{Z}^{2}
corresponds to the unique slope
r/s\in\widehat{\mathbb{Q}}
, and every slope in
\widehat{\mathbb{Q}}
arises from a unique primitive element of
2\mathbb{Z}^{2}
in this form.

As before, define
\omega\coloneq s+ir
and
\widetilde{\omega}\coloneq-p+iq
, where
p,q\in\mathbb{Z}

are chosen so that
pr+qs=1
.
We know that
\xi\coloneq\wp(\ell_{r/s}(0))
and

\xi^{\prime}\coloneq\wp(\ell_{r/s}(\widetilde{\omega}))
are disjoint
geodesic arcs in
(\mathbb{P},V)
. The sets
\wp^{-1}(\xi)
and
\wp^{-1}(\xi^{\prime})
consist of parallel lines with slope
r/s
, and these lines alternate in the following sense: each component of
\mathbb{C}\setminus\wp^{-1}(\xi\cup\xi^{\prime})
is an infinite strip whose boundary contains one line in
\wp^{-1}(\xi)
and one line in
\wp^{-1}(\xi^{\prime})
.

We may assume that
\gamma
is in minimal
position with
\xi\cup\xi^{\prime}
. Moreover, we may assume that the parametrization
\beta
of
\gamma
as a simple loop was chosen so that
\beta(0)=\beta(1)\not\in\xi\cup\xi^{\prime}
.

Then by Lemmas
10.1
and
10.3
we know that either
\xi\cap\gamma=\emptyset=\xi^{\prime}\cap\gamma
, or the following conditions are true:
\gamma
meets
\xi
and
\xi^{\prime}
transversely, the sets
\xi\cap\gamma
and
\xi^{\prime}\cap\gamma
are non-empty and finite, and the points in these sets alternate on
\gamma
.
We claim that the latter is not possible.

Otherwise, we choose a lift
\alpha
of
\beta
under
\wp
. Then
\alpha
intersects the lines in
\wp^{-1}(\xi\cup\xi^{\prime})
transversely,
we have

k\coloneq\#(\alpha\cap\wp^{-1}(\xi))=\#(\alpha\cap\wp^{-1}(\xi^{\prime}))\in\mathbb{N},

and the points in
\alpha\cap\wp^{-1}(\xi)\neq\emptyset
and
\alpha\cap\wp^{-1}(\xi^{\prime})\neq\emptyset
alternate on
\alpha
. An argument very similar to the proof of Lemma
10.4
then shows that
2k>0
is the number of lines in the set
\wp^{-1}(\xi\cup\xi^{\prime})
that separate
\alpha(0)
from

\alpha(1)
. On the other hand, we know that
\alpha(1)-\alpha(0)=2(s+ir)
and so
\alpha(1)
and
\alpha(0)
lie on a line with slope
r/s
and are not separated by any line in
\wp^{-1}(\xi\cup\xi^{\prime})
. This is a contradiction.

This shows that
\xi\cap\gamma=\emptyset=\xi^{\prime}\cap\gamma
. Now consider a simple closed geodesic
\tau_{r/s}=\wp(\ell_{r/s}(t\widetilde{\omega}))
with slope
r/s
and
0<t<1
. Since
\xi\cap\gamma=\emptyset
, we can choose

t
very close
to
0
so that
\tau_{r/s}\cap\gamma=\emptyset
.
Now we can apply considerations very similar to the proof of Corollary
3.5

(ii)
. The complement of

\tau_{r/s}\cup\gamma
in
\mathbb{P}
is a
disjoint union
\mathbb{P}\setminus(\tau_{r/s}\cup\gamma)=W\cup U\cup W^{\prime}
,
where
W,W^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{P}
are open Jordan regions and
U\subset\mathbb{P}
is an annulus with
\partial U=\tau_{r/s}\cup\gamma
.
Since
\tau_{r/s}
and
\gamma
are essential, both
W
and
W^{\prime}
must contain at least two points in
V
. Since
\#V=4
, we have

U\cap V=\emptyset
. Lemma
2.1

now implies that

\tau_{r/s}
and
\gamma
are isotopic rel.
V
. By Lemma
10.7

the curve
\gamma
is actually isotopic to
each
closed geodesic
\tau_{r/s}
with slope
r/s
.

The map
[\gamma]\mapsto r/s
that sends each isotopy class
[\gamma]
to a slope
r/s\in\widehat{\mathbb{Q}}
obtained from a primitive element in
2\mathbb{Z}^{2}
associated with
[\gamma]
according to Lemma
10.6
is well-defined. It is clear that it is surjective, because the isotopy class
[\tau_{r/s}]
of a geodesic
\tau_{r/s}
with slope
r/s\in\widehat{\mathbb{Q}}
is sent to
r/s
. To see that it is injective, suppose two isotopy
classes
[\gamma]
and
[\gamma^{\prime}]
are sent to the same slope
r/s\in\widehat{\mathbb{Q}}
by this map. If
\tau_{r/s}
is a closed geodesic with slope
r/s
, then by our previous discussion we have

\gamma\sim\tau_{r/s}\sim\gamma^{\prime}
rel.
V
.
Hence
[\gamma]=[\gamma^{\prime}]
. It follows that the map
[\gamma]\mapsto r/s
is indeed a bijection.
∎

Remark 10.8
.

Suppose
\gamma
is an essential Jordan curve in
(\mathbb{P},V)
and its isotopy class
[\gamma]
rel.
V

corresponds to slope

r/s\in\widehat{\mathbb{Q}}
according to Lemma
2.3
. Let
\beta\colon\mathbb{I}\rightarrow\gamma

be a parametrization of
\gamma
as a simple loop, and

\alpha\colon\mathbb{I}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
be a lift of
\beta
under
\wp
. Then (
10.4
) in the proof of Lemma
10.6
shows that we always have
\alpha(1)-\alpha(0)=\pm 2(s+ir)
.

A similar statement is true for arcs in
(\mathbb{P},V)
.

Corollary 10.9
.

Let
\xi
be an arc in
(\mathbb{P},V)
. Then
\xi
is isotopic rel.
V
to a geodesic arc
\xi_{r/s}
for some slope
r/s\in\widehat{\mathbb{Q}}
. Moreover, if
\beta\colon\mathbb{I}\rightarrow\xi
is a homeomorphic parametrization of
\xi
and
\alpha
is any lift of

\beta
under
\wp
, then
\alpha(1)-\alpha(0)=\pm(s+ir)
.

Proof.

The arc
\xi
joins two of the points in
V
, while the other two points in
V
do not lie on
\xi
. Hence we may “surround”
\xi
by an essential Jordan curve
\gamma
in
(\mathbb{P},V)
such that
\xi
is a core arc of
\gamma
. By Lemma
2.3
we know that
\gamma
is isotopic rel.
V
to a closed geodesic
\tau_{r/s}
in
\mathbb{P}
with some slope
r/s\in\widehat{\mathbb{Q}}
. Hence
\xi
is isotopic rel.
V
to a core arc
\xi^{\prime}
of
\tau_{r/s}
.

Now any two arcs in the interior of a closed topological disk
D
with the same endpoints are isotopic by an isotopy that fixes the endpoints of these arcs and the points in
\partial D
(see
[
Bus10
, Theorem A.6 (ii)]
). This implies that any two core arcs of an essential Jordan curve in
(\mathbb{P},V)
are isotopic rel.
V
if the core arcs have the same endpoints.
We know that the closed geodesic
\tau_{r/s}
has precisely two
geodesic arcs with slope
r/s
as core arcs in different components of
\mathbb{P}\setminus\tau_{r/s}
. Therefore,
\xi^{\prime}
, and hence also
\xi
, is isotopic rel.
V
to a geodesic arc
\xi_{r/s}
with slope
r/s
. This proves the first part of the statement.

Let
\beta\colon\mathbb{I}\rightarrow\xi
be a homeomorphic parametrization
of
\xi
and
\alpha\colon\mathbb{I}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
be a lift of
\beta
under
\wp
.
By what we have seen, we can choose an isotopy
H\colon\mathbb{P}\times\mathbb{I}\rightarrow\mathbb{P}
rel.
V
with
H_{0}=\operatorname{id}_{\mathbb{P}}
and
H_{1}(\xi)=\xi_{r/s}
.
We use this to define a homotopy
\overline{H}\colon\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I}\rightarrow\mathbb{P}
by setting

\overline{H}(u,t)\coloneqq H(\beta(u),t)

for
u,t\in\mathbb{I}
.
Note that
\overline{H}_{t}
for
t\in\mathbb{I}
gives a homeomorphic parametrization
of an arc in
(\mathbb{P},V)
. These arcs have all the same endpoints. In particular
u\in\mathbb{I}\mapsto\beta^{\prime}(u)\coloneqq\overline{H}_{1}(u)=H_{1}(\beta(u))
gives a homeomorphic parametrization of
H_{1}(\xi)=\xi_{r/s}
.

We can lift
\overline{H}_{t}
under
\wp
to find a homotopy

\widetilde{H}\colon\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
such that
\widetilde{H}_{0}=\alpha

and
\wp\circ\widetilde{H}_{t}=\overline{H}_{t}

for all
t\in\mathbb{I}
. To see this, one first applies the standard homotopy lifting theorem (see
[
Hat02
, Proposition 1.30]
) to the homotopy
\overline{H}
restricted
(0,1)\times\mathbb{I}
and the covering map
\wp\colon\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{Z}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{P}\setminus V\supset\overline{H}((0,1)\times\mathbb{I})

to obtain a unique homotopy
\widetilde{H}\colon(0,1)\times\mathbb{I}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
with
\widetilde{H}_{0}=\alpha|(0,1)
. Now as
u_{0}\to 0^{+}
, the set

\overline{H}((0,u_{0}]\times\mathbb{I})
shrinks to the point
\beta(0)\in V
, and so the connected set
\widetilde{H}((0,u_{0}]\times\mathbb{I})

shrinks to a unique point in
\wp^{-1}(V)=\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. This point can only be
\alpha(0)
. Hence
\overline{H}(u,t)\to\alpha(0)
uniformly for
t\in\mathbb{I}
as
u\to 0^{+}
, and similarly
\overline{H}(u,t)\to\alpha(1)

uniformly for
t\in\mathbb{I}
as
u\to 1^{-}
.
This implies that we can continuously extend

\widetilde{H}
to a homotopy on
\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I}
with the desired properties by setting
\widetilde{H}(0,t)=\alpha(0)
and

\widetilde{H}(1,t)=\alpha(1)
for
t\in\mathbb{I}
.

Then
\alpha^{\prime}\coloneq\widetilde{H}_{1}
is a lift of
\beta^{\prime}
, because

\wp\circ\alpha^{\prime}=\wp\circ\widetilde{H}_{1}=\overline{H}_{1}=\beta^{\prime}
.
Since
\beta^{\prime}
is a homeomorphic parametrization of the geodesic arc
\xi_{r/s}
, the path
\alpha^{\prime}
sends
\mathbb{I}
homeomorphically onto a subsegment of
a line
\ell_{r/s}\subset\mathbb{C}
. Since
\alpha^{\prime}
has its endpoints in
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and

\alpha^{\prime}((0,1))
is disjoint from
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, this implies

\alpha^{\prime}(1)-\alpha^{\prime}(0)=\pm(s+ir)
.
Since
\alpha
and
\alpha^{\prime}
have the same endpoints, the statement follows. ∎

Proof of Lemma
2.4
.

In the proof all isotopies, isotopy classes, intersection numbers, etc. are for isotopies on
\mathbb{P}
rel.
V
. We will use the facts about the geodesics on
(\mathbb{P},V)
discussed before Lemma
10.7
without further reference.

(i)
Let
\alpha
and
\beta
be essential Jordan curves in
(\mathbb{P},V)
as in the statement. As before, we define
\omega=s+ir
and

\widetilde{\omega}=-p+iq
, where
p,q\in\mathbb{Z}
and
pr+qs=1
.

First suppose that
r/s=r^{\prime}/s^{\prime}
. Then in the isotopy class
[\alpha]=[\beta]
we can find simple closed geodesics with slope

r/s
that are disjoint, for example the curves

\tau_{r/s}=\wp(\ell_{r/s}(\widetilde{\omega}/3))
and

\tau^{\prime}_{r/s}=\wp(\ell_{r/s}(2\widetilde{\omega}/3))
. It follows that

\mathrm{i}(\alpha,\beta)=0
in this case.

We now assume that
r/s\neq r^{\prime}/s^{\prime}
. In order to determine
\mathrm{i}(\alpha,\beta)
, we have to find the minimum of all numbers

\#(\alpha\cap\beta)
, where
\alpha
and
\beta
range over the given isotopy classes. By applying a suitable isotopy, we can reduce to the case where
\alpha
is a fixed curve in its isotopy class and we only have to take variations over
\beta
. So by Lemma
2.3
we may assume that

\alpha=\tau_{r/s}=\wp(\ell_{r/s})
is a simple closed geodesic as in the statement. Then
\tau_{r/s}=\wp(\ell_{r/s}(t_{0}\widetilde{\omega}))
for some
t_{0}\in(0,1)
. The preimage

\wp^{-1}(\tau_{r/s})
of
\tau_{r/s}
under
\wp
consists of the two disjoint families

(10.5)

\mathcal{F}_{1}\coloneq\{\ell_{r/s}((t_{0}+2j)\widetilde{\omega}):j\in\mathbb{Z}\}\text{ and }\mathcal{F}_{2}\coloneq\{\ell_{r/s}((-t_{0}+2j)\widetilde{\omega}):j\in\mathbb{Z}\}

of distinct lines with slope
r/s
.

Now let
\widetilde{\beta}\colon\mathbb{I}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
be a lift of
\beta
under
\wp
,
where we think of
\beta
as a simple closed loop in a parametrization with suitable orientation.
Then if
z_{0}\coloneq\widetilde{\beta}(0)
and
w_{0}\coloneq\widetilde{\beta}(1)
, we have
w_{0}-z_{0}=2(s^{\prime}+ir^{\prime})
as follows from
Remark
10.8
. By changing the basepoint of
\beta
if neccesary, we may assume that

\widetilde{\beta}(0),\widetilde{\beta}(1)\not\in\wp^{-1}(\tau_{r/s})
. If
\omega^{\prime}\coloneq s^{\prime}+ir^{\prime}
, then we can write
\omega^{\prime}

uniquely in the form

(10.6)

\omega^{\prime}=k\omega+n\widetilde{\omega},

where
k,n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Note that then
|n|=N\coloneq|rs^{\prime}-sr^{\prime}|>0
(to see this, multiply (
10.6
) by the complex conjugate of
\omega
and take imaginary parts).

Now each family
\mathcal{F}_{j}
,
j=1,2
, consists of equally spaced parallel lines with slope
r/s
such that consecutive lines in each family differ by a translation by
2\widetilde{\omega}
. This implies that the points
z_{0}
and
w_{0}=z_{0}+2\omega^{\prime}
are separated by precisely
N
lines from each of the families
\mathcal{F}_{j}
,
j=1,2
.
So
\widetilde{\beta}
must have at least
2N
points in common with

\wp^{-1}(\tau_{r/s})
. Since
\wp\circ\widetilde{\beta}
maps
[0,1)
injectively onto
\beta
, we conclude that
\beta=\wp(\widetilde{\beta})
has at least
2N
points in common with
\tau_{r/s}
. If
\beta=\tau_{r^{\prime}/s^{\prime}}
, then
\widetilde{\beta}
is a parametrization of the line segment
[z_{0},w_{0}]
, and so
\widetilde{\beta}
meets

\wp^{-1}(\tau_{r/s})
in precisely
2N
points. This means that
\tau_{r/s}
and
\tau_{r^{\prime}/s^{\prime}}
have exactly
2N
points in common.

It follows that for all
\beta
we have

2N\leq\#(\wp^{-1}(\tau_{r/s})\cap\widetilde{\beta})=\#(\tau_{r/s}\cap\beta),

and so
2N\leq\mathrm{i}(\alpha,\beta)\leq\#(\tau_{r/s}\cap\tau_{r^{\prime}/s^{\prime}})=2N.

Thus we have equality here and the statement follows.

(ii)
This is a variant of the argument in
(i)
and we use the same notation.

Since
\beta\sim\tau_{r^{\prime}/s^{\prime}}
, the core arc

\xi
of
\beta
is isotopic to a core arc of
\tau_{r^{\prime}/s^{\prime}}
.
Now two core arcs of a given essential Jordan curve in
(\mathbb{P},V)
are isotopic rel.
V
if they have the same endpoints (this was pointed out in the proof of Corollary
10.9
). It follows that
\xi\sim\xi^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}/s^{\prime}}
, where
\xi^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}/s^{\prime}}
is one of the two
geodesic core arcs of
\tau_{r^{\prime}/s^{\prime}}
. In particular,

\xi^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}/s^{\prime}}=\wp(\ell_{r^{\prime}/s^{\prime}})
for a line
\ell_{r^{\prime}/s^{\prime}}\subset\mathbb{C}
that contains a point in
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. Note that
\xi^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}/s^{\prime}}
possibly differs from the geodesic arc
\xi_{r^{\prime}/s^{\prime}}
as in the statement (if
\xi^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}/s^{\prime}}
and
\xi_{r^{\prime}/s^{\prime}}
lie in different components of
\mathbb{P}\setminus\tau_{r^{\prime}/s^{\prime}}
).

If
\widetilde{\xi}\colon\mathbb{I}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
is a lift of
\xi
under
\wp
in suitable orientation, and
z_{1}\coloneq\widetilde{\xi}(0)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
,
w_{1}\coloneq\widetilde{\xi}(1)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, then it follows from Corollary
10.9
that
w_{1}-z_{1}=\omega^{\prime}=s^{\prime}+ir^{\prime}
.
Now (
10.6
) implies that there are exactly
N=|n|=|rs^{\prime}-sr^{\prime}|
lines
in
\mathcal{F}_{1}\cup\mathcal{F}_{2}
that separate
z_{1}
and
w_{1}

(essentially, this follows from the fact that the set
[0,n]\cap\{2j\pm t_{0}:j\in\mathbb{Z}\}
, where
t_{0}\in(0,1)
, contains precisely
N=|n|
points).

Let
\widetilde{\omega}^{\prime}\coloneq-p^{\prime}+iq^{\prime}
, where
p^{\prime},q^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
and
p^{\prime}r^{\prime}+q^{\prime}s^{\prime}=1
. Then, by the discussion before Lemma
10.7
, the map
\wp
sends one of the segments
[z_{1},w_{1}]
and
[z_{1}+\widetilde{\omega}^{\prime},w_{1}+\widetilde{\omega}^{\prime}]

homeomorphically onto
\xi_{r^{\prime}/s^{\prime}}
depending on whether
\xi^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}/s^{\prime}}=\xi_{r^{\prime}/s^{\prime}}
or
\xi^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}/s^{\prime}}\neq\xi_{r^{\prime}/s^{\prime}}
, respectively. In either case, each segment meets exactly
N
lines
in
\mathcal{F}_{1}\cup\mathcal{F}_{2}
.

Arguing as before, we see that

\#(\tau_{r/s}\cap\xi_{r^{\prime}/s^{\prime}})=N\leq\#(\wp^{-1}(\tau_{r/s})\cap\widetilde{\xi})=\#(\tau_{r/s}\cap\xi).

This leads to

N\leq\mathrm{i}(\alpha,\xi)\leq\#(\tau_{r/s}\cap\xi_{r^{\prime}/s^{\prime}})=N.

So we have equality here and the statement follows.

(iii)
–
(v)
These are special cases of

(i)
and
(ii)
.
For example,
a=\wp(\mathbb{R}\times\{0\})=\wp(\ell_{0}(0))
is a core arc of
\alpha^{h}
corresponding to slope
r^{\prime}/s^{\prime}=0/1=0
. Hence by
(ii)
we have

\mathrm{i}(\alpha,a)=|r\cdot 1-s\cdot 0|=|r|=\#(\tau_{r/s}\cap\xi_{0})=\#(\tau_{r/s}\cap a).

The other statements follow from similar considerations.
∎
